 - Threat Model
Generated: 7/14/2026
Overview
STRIDE Framework
STRIDE is a threat modeling framework developed by Microsoft, designed to help identify and classify potential security threats through six categories: Spoofing (impersonation), Tampering (unauthorized modification), Repudiation (deniability of actions), Information Disclosure (unauthorized access to data), Denial of Service (availability loss), and Elevation of Privilege (gaining unauthorized higher privileges). Applying STRIDE to an architecture helps teams systematically review trust boundaries, data flows, and security controls.
Industry-Specific Threats
Organizations in the technology sector are particularly vulnerable to threats targeting intellectual property, customer PII, payment card information, and employee credentials. State-sponsored actors are primarily interested in stealing technical intellectual property through sophisticated espionage attacks, while cybercriminals focus on extracting valuable data such as IP and financial records. Hacktivists and competitors aim to compromise social media accounts and damage the organization's reputation. Technology firms must be vigilant against ransomware attacks, which are highly profitable and increasingly prevalent due to the rise of Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) models. These models allow cybercriminals to quickly rebrand and avoid law enforcement, maintaining an average lifespan of 17 months. Additionally, Google Ad fraud attacks distributing malware like Batloader and IcedID pose a significant threat, as do Qakbot phishing campaigns linked to multiple ransomware families, including Conti and Black Basta.

When conducting a threat modeling exercise, organizations should prioritize the protection of intellectual property, customer PII, and payment card information. They should watch for sophisticated phishing campaigns, particularly those involving Qakbot, which often precede ransomware attacks. The rapid evolution and rebranding of ransomware groups highlight the need for robust detection and response mechanisms. Moreover, organizations should be on high alert for malware distributed through Google Ads and monitor for unusual activities that may indicate the presence of backdoors or credential harvesting tools. Integrating multi-factor authentication, regularly updating phishing training programs, and encouraging a culture of reporting suspicious activity are essential steps in building a resilient defense against these threats. Third-party risk management and regular audits of security practices among vendors are also crucial to mitigate potential entry points for threat actors.
System Overview
The system architecture primarily revolves around a processing flow that handles submissions through final delivery, with a significant involvement of an AI pipeline. Major components include various AI agents, workflow states, and interaction points such as Slack, Jira Cloud, and IBM's consulting platforms. The system enforces a trust boundary through passcode protections for most parts, except for specific callbacks and package pulls, indicating potential security vulnerabilities in these areas. The workflow state is managed using local JSON files, which may pose risks due to potential exposure of sensitive information if not properly secured.

Trusted components within the system include the AI pipeline, consisting of three Granite agents, and the interaction with IBM's consulting tools such as WatsonX.ai and IBM Context Studio. These components drive the core functionality of the system, facilitating the automation and intelligence needed for efficient processing. Additionally, the architecture uses a legend node to define styling and shorthands for visual elements in the diagrams, aiding in the clear representation of trust boundaries and authenticated connections without detailing the actual boundaries. This approach helps in maintaining a cohesive and understandable system overview, although it abstracts critical security details.

Sensitive data flows are primarily managed within internal workflows and local storage, raising concerns about potential breaches. Slack callbacks and calls to the IBM Consulting Advantage (ICA) could expose sensitive information if not adequately secured. This indicates a need for robust security measures to protect data integrity and confidentiality throughout the system's lifecycle. The tension noted is the lack of detailed trust boundary visualizations in the legend, which, although it aids in simplicity, may overlook critical security representations. Overall, the architecture requires stringent security protocols, particularly around sensitive data handling and boundary management, to mitigate potential threats effectively.

Threat Statement
External actor forges authentication tokens in the Slack callback which results in impersonating a legitimate consultant to gain unauthorized access and perform tasks
Risk Justification
Slack callback interception and token forgery can occur if HMACToken validation is weak, allowing for compromised slacks and task manipulation
Impact
Unauthorized actions on behalf of the consultant, potentially leading to misleading tasks completion actions and data integrity breaches
High-Level Mitigation
Implement strong crypto token validation techniques, such as HMAC-SHA256, encrypted with AES-256, ensuring challenge and response mechanisms are in place

Threat Statement
External actor forger forged TLS certificates which results in conducting man-in-the-middle attacks on the ICA package pull process

Threat Statement
External threat actor forges authentication tokens for the AI pipeline which results in impersonating legitimate agents to gain unauthorized access to the AI processing environment
Risk Justification
Inadequate token validation and absence of proper cryptographic measures enable the forgery of session tokens. Weak secrets and inadequate access controls increase the risk
Impact
Unauthorized access to multiple stages of the AI pipeline, manipulation of AI outcomes, and potential data exfiltration or tampering leading to fraudulent decisions or outcomes
High-Level Mitigation
Implement JWT with HMAC SHA-256 or RS256 and enforce strict expiration policies with refresh tokens. Use mutual TLS for secure agent communication and enable time-based one-time passwords (TOTP) for additional verification

Threat Statement
External threat actor forges HMAC tokens in the Slack callback which results in impersonating authorized Slack users to mark tasks as done
Risk Justification
If an attacker can successfully reverse-engineer or intercept valid HMAC tokens, they can send spoofed requests to the "Mark Done" endpoint. Weak HMAC token secrecy or algorithmic weaknesses can enable this spoofing.
Impact
Unauthorized marking of tasks as completed, leading to incomplete work being reported as done, and possible financial loss due to missed tasks and opportunities
High-Level Mitigation
Implement robust HMAC key management and use strong cryptographic algorithms (e.g., HMAC-SHA512). Define rigorous access controls for task management responses and ensure all APIs are authenticated and authorized.

External actor creates fake SMTP email headers in the Email (SMTP) service which results in phishing or spoofing legitimate task notifications

Privilege escalation in email systems allows an attacker to spoof email senders resulting in fabricated task emails sent to users

Threat Statement
A criminal actor spoofs an API gateway by intercepting and modifying the query request to IBM Context Studio (MCP) which results in manipulating contextual assessments
Risk Justification

AT-3(4): Role-based Training | Suspicious Communications and Anomalous System Behavior (CWE-359)
IA-2(9): Identification and Authentication (Organizational Users) | Network Access to Non-privileged Accounts â€” Replay Resistant (CWE-359)
Threat Statement
Attacker forged SSL/TLS certificates in a man-in-the-middle attack on the wovenx calls which results in intercepting request and response payloads between the Granite agents and Watsonx
Risk Justification
Insufficient certificate validation within the 2.0 AI pipeline (3 Granite agents) can allow an attacker to present self-signed or misissued certificates
Impact

High-Level Mitigation
Implement certificate pinning for client-server communications, use certificate transparency logs, and mandate certificate-based mutual TLS for high-sensitivity endpoints

Threat Statement
External attacker intercepts email traffic between servers which results in modifying content of emails associated with task assignments
Risk Justification
Email communications occur over unencrypted SMTP connections or using outdated TLS versions, such as TLS 1.0, making them susceptible to man-in-the-middle (MITM) attacks.
Impact

High-Level Mitigation

Threat Statement
Authorized consultant User tampers with Slack's 'Mark Done' feature communication messages which results in misreporting task completion to the workflow state

Threat Statement
Malicious insider deletes steps in the local JSON file of the workflow state which results in proving non-existence of specific transactions to company auditors
Risk Justification
The local JSON file lacks immutable logging mechanisms and file integrity checks facilitating tampering of recorded workflow state changes.

Threat 156347
Node: D1 Workflow state (local JSON file)
Category: Repudiation
Perspective: conventional_IT
MITRE ATT&CK: T1070.006: Indicator Removal: Timestomp
CAPEC/CWE: CAPEC-268 (CWE-117)
NIST Controls: AC-7(1): Unsuccessful Logon Attempts | Automatic Account Lock (CWE-117)
AU-2(4): Event Logging | Privileged Functions (CWE-117)
CM-11(1): User-installed Software | Alerts for Unauthorized Installations (CWE-117)
... +2 more controls
Threat Statement
An attacker modifies timestamps in the local JSON file which results in creating plausible deniability for any unauthorized modification of critical process steps
Risk Justification
The current system lacks cryptographic verification of timestamps, allowing manipulation. Additionally, the file storage permissions might be overly permissive allowing modification without proper authorization checks.
Impact
Unreliable timestamps prevent traceability of event sequences, making it difficult to prove system integrity during incident response
High-Level Mitigation
Use synchronized Network Time Protocol (NTP) for accurate timestamps and integrate Time Stamp Authority (TSA) to provide digitally signed timestamps for integrity verification

Threat 156350
Node: D1 Workflow state (local JSON file)
Category: Repudiation
Perspective: conventional_IT
MITRE ATT&CK: T1222: File and Directory Permissions Modification
CAPEC/CWE: No CAPEC mappings found for primary technique: T1222
NIST Controls: AC-4(23): Information Flow Enforcement | Modify Non-releasable Information
SC-30(4): Concealment and Misdirection | Misleading Information
AC-4(15): Information Flow Enforcement | Detection of Unsanctioned Information
Threat Statement
Malicious insider modifies workflow statuses in the JSON file which results in concealing evidence of completed tasks
Risk Justification
File-level access controls insufficient to prevent privileged users from altering the file content without detection. There is no auditing of access or modification in place for this file.
Impact
The management cannot accurately verify workflow statuses, compromising decision-making and potential regulatory non-compliance
High-Level Mitigation
Implement file integrity monitoring (FIM) with SHA-256 hashes and file change auditing. Use role-based access controls (RBAC) and enforce principle of least privilege

Threat Statement
Malicious insider clears audit logs in the Langflow logging service which results in concealing unauthorized changes to task assignments

Threat Statement
External attacker intercepts emails in transit using packet capture techniques which results in exposing sensitive task information
Risk Justification
Emails can contain sensitive data, and unencrypted transmission can be intercepted, as SMTP without encryption exposes data to man-in-the-middle attacks
Impact
Unauthorized access to task details, leading to potential data breaches and disclosure of confidential information
High-Level Mitigation

Threat Statement
External attacker exploits misconfigured permissions in Slack APIs which results in accessing and viewing private Slack messages

Malicious attacker exploits horizontal privilege escalation to access peer-level resources by forging API session tokens in the JSON network flow which results in accessing shared files in the local JSON file

Summary
Executive Summary
The information technology system under review operates within a highly dynamic and interconnected environment, characterized by the pervasive use of digital technologies and cloud-based services. The system's operational landscape includes a variety of applications, network components, and user interfaces, all of which are subject to a continually evolving threat landscape. Key findings indicate significant vulnerabilities across multiple STRIDE categories, including spoofing, tampering, and information disclosure. The system's authentication mechanisms and data encryption practices are pivotal areas requiring immediate attention to mitigate potential exploitation by adversaries. The threat model revealed substantial risk exposures, particularly in the realm of Denial of Service and Elevation of Privilege. The overall risk posture is deemed high due to the intricate interplay of these system components and the sophistication of potential attacks. Strategic recommendations aim to fortify the system's defensive posture through comprehensive security controls, regular audits, and adherence to industry standards such as NIST and ISO 27001. Operational resilience is essential to ensure ongoing business continuity.
Overall Prioritized Recommendations
1. **Strengthen Security Monitoring:** Deploy advanced Security Information and Event Management (SIEM) systems to enhance real-time threat detection and incident response capabilities.
2. **Adopt a Zero-Trust Architecture:** Transition towards a Zero-Trust architecture to minimize the attack surface by verifying trust at every stage of communication.
3. **Enhanced User Training:** Roll out comprehensive security awareness training programs for all employees and stakeholders to mitigate social engineering and phishing attacks.
4. **Compliance with Industry Standards:** Ensure the system adheres to NIST and ISO 27001 standards, ensuring a robust security framework and best practices.
5. **Data Encryption:** Implement end-to-end encryption for all sensitive data in transit and at rest to protect against unauthorized access and theft.
6. **Network Segmentation:** Deploy network segmentation to isolate critical components and limit the lateral movement of potential attackers.
7. **Incident Response Plan:** Develop and regularly update an incident response plan to efficiently manage and mitigate security breaches.
8. **Least Privilege Principle:** Enforce the principle of least privilege, ensuring that users and systems have the minimum levels of access necessary for their functions.
9. **Regular Security Reviews:** Perform regular security reviews and risk assessments to adapt to the changing threat landscape and improve security defenses.
10. **Third-Party Risk Management:** Implement a stringent third-party risk management program to assess and mitigate vulnerabilities introduced by external vendors and service providers.
